That fails closed on trust — and it is a *silent, unrequested change to a trust boundary*, which is the one category of change this entire ADR exists to prevent. **The house line is already drawn exactly here and I am following it, not inventing it:** `scripts/framework/machine-accounts.env` (consumer-specific config) is deliberately absent from that list while `protected_surfaces.txt` (HOS-owned policy) is present at `:57` — both verified by me. F6 already makes an absent roster benign: empty set, gate runs, CODEOWNERS and apps still confer trust (FR3).
>
> **The reason must travel with the list entry, not only with this document.** Amendment 3's own risk register names *"the roster being added to `framework_consumer_files.txt` by a later contributor for completeness"* as a way this ruling gets silently undone. **Binding: the two added lines carry a comment in the file stating why the roster is absent** — one sentence, in the same register as the file's existing section comments. A decision recorded only in a design document is a decision the next contributor will not find.
>
> **3. A `.example` template, if one is shipped at all, MUST be at a different path.** Never the live one. I rule that **one is shipped**: `scripts/framework/trusted-requesters.txt.example`, added to the ship list, carrying §1.3's format and the same header the live file carries. The alternative — documenting the format only in prose — makes a consumer hand-construct a file whose parser rejects malformed lines silently-but-for-a-diagnostic, and the format is exactly the kind of thing `.example` files exist for. The consumer-facing documentation of the roster is required either way (§4.3).
>
> **4. S2 creates a new consumer precondition, and nothing states it.** Step B1/B3 exit 2 on an absent or empty `machine-accounts.env`, and that file is **not shipped and not generated by the installer** — verified by me: it is absent from `framework_consumer_files.txt`, and `provision_agent_account.sh` *dies* if it is missing. **Today a consumer can run cron cycles without it** (it is needed only by the merge gates). **After S2, its absence stops work selection entirely.** That is a real change to the consumer contract. It fails closed and it is visible via DEV-2 — but it must be **written down** rather than discovered by a consumer whose worker goes quiet. §4.3 places it in two homes: the **S2 work item (#1539)** and the **consumer-facing upgrade note in the S2 PR body**.
>
> **And the detector, because P-4's real lesson is that nothing would have caught this.** The existing guard test checks only that *listed* files exist, so it cannot see a listed file whose **dependency** is unlisted. **§6.3 gains a test asserting that every `scripts/framework/**` module imported by a shipped module is itself shipped** — for this change set at minimum, `select_work_candidates.py` → `requester_trust.py` → `require_human_approval.py`. Without it, P-4 recurs the next time a shipped module grows an import, and it recurs invisibly from this repo.

> **Ruled: the count is FIVE (AM-17 — §8/E-6 UPHELD in full).** Revision 2's enumeration above was already correct, so **revision 3 ratifies it rather than changing it**, and deliberately does not re-derive it: the surface list is checked against `scripts/framework/protected_surfaces.txt`, and that check was made by revision 2 and re-made by the architect. Three things the ruling settles, each of which was an open question in revision 2: (i) **S2 does touch `.github/workflows/**`** — AM-6 point 3 is a shipping condition and the confirmation comment the workflow posts is the artefact that violates it, so the edit is in scope; (ii) **S2 is not blocked** by the extra surface — the architect declined the "then S2 does not ship" branch explicitly, because blocking a `priority:critical` fix over a one-surface enumeration would be worse than either the fix or the enumeration; (iii) **there is no sixth** — `CLAUDE.md` (option D) stays out of S1/S2 for the reason §2.7.1 gives, which the architect confirmed, and is named to the human as a **planned follow-up** so a sixth surface arriving later is expected rather than a surprise. The correction to what the human is asked to clear is made in **Amendment 2 §3(b) item 4**, which is the text that must reach them; Amendment 1's four-surface text is superseded and must not be quoted.

### 2.2 CLI contract

```
python3 -m scripts.framework.select_work_candidates \
    --repo <owner/repo> \
    --milestone <positive integer> \
    [--label <name>]                        # default: needs-ai
    [--max-candidates <n>]                  # NEW in revision 4 (AM-19). Sufficiency
                                            #   stop. Default 10; may only lower.
    [--max-authorization-checks <n>]        # REDEFINED in revision 4 (AM-19). A COST
                                            #   CEILING, not a coverage budget.
                                            #   Default 100; may only lower.
```

**stdout** — zero or more lines, byte-identical in format to the deleted jq filter:

```
#<number> [<critical|high|medium|low>] <title>
```

**stderr** — diagnostics and exactly one summary line. Never candidate data.

**Exit codes** — two, and only two:

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `0` | The determination completed **to the extent the gate can state**. stdout is an exact **prefix** of the complete answer (§2.3), and the summary line's `complete=<yes\|no>` says whether it is the whole of it. |
| `2` | **Fail closed.** stdout is empty. stderr carries one `select_work_candidates: <reason-token>` line. The caller must treat this as "no candidates", never as "unknown, proceed anyway". |

`argparse`'s own usage-error exit is also `2`, which is consistent: a malformed invocation is a fail-closed condition.

**The criterion that decides which of the two a bound produces (AM-21, binding — new in revision 4).** Revision 3 argued both sides of this without ever writing down the principle, and the panel was right that §2.3 contradicted itself: D5.3 exited 2 on a failed events query *because "a partial candidate list is a correctness hazard — it can silently demote a `priority:critical` item"*, while D5.1 presented a partial determination as complete with exit 0 **and demoted a `priority:critical` item**. Both sentences were defensible locally; neither was written against the other. The resolution is not to pick a side — it is to state the property that distinguishes them:

> **A bound may produce a partial determination with exit 0 only when (i) the omission is *enumerable* — the gate can state how many records it did not evaluate and why — and (ii) the omission is *ordered* — it falls at the tail of an order the gate itself controls, so nothing omitted can outrank anything emitted. If either property is absent, the determination is unknown in extent and the gate MUST exit 2.**

This is now the general rule rather than a local argument, and §3's matrix is read against it.

#### 2.2.1 The gate's three bounds (AM-22, binding — new in revision 4)

Revision 3's authorization-check cap **had no FR and no AD**. AD-10's per-cycle cap is the *assessor's* (ESC-5); this one was introduced by this document at self-rated MEDIUM confidence, "not derived from measurement", and was never put to the human — while Amendment 2 §3(b) H1 item 2 assured the human that the queue's true size *"is knowable only by running the gate, which is what S2 ships."* **That assurance was not true under a cap of 25**: a gate that evaluates 25 of 97 records cannot report the queue's size. That is the sharpest of the panel's three aggravators, because it is the point at which a design-stage number reached the human's clearance text wearing an architect's authority. The bounds now have an architectural home, and **each cites the ruling that authorizes it and the property that makes its truncation safe** (AM-23's extension of AR-5).

| Bound | Value | What it bounds | What it may truncate | Safe because | Report | Authorized by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **List pages** (Step C) | `--paginate`, **5 pages / 500 records**. Not a flag — a module constant. | The candidate **set** | **Unordered** — `created desc`, so the **oldest** records | **It is not.** Enumerable but *not* ordered: a `priority:critical` record can be in the dropped region. It exits 0 anyway, under protest — see below | `WARN candidate-list-truncated` — **the loudest class**, plus `complete=no` | **AM-22 / AF-8** |
| **Authorization cost ceiling** | **100** checks. `--max-authorization-checks`, **may only lower**: `min(100, max(0, provided))` | The **cost** of the determination | The **ranked tail** (AM-19) | Enumerable **and** ordered. Nothing omitted can outrank anything emitted | `WARN authorization-cost-ceiling-reached evaluated=<E> unevaluated=<U>`, plus `complete=no` (AM-20 point 5) | **AM-19 / AM-21** |
| **Events pages per issue** (D5.3) | 10 pages / 1000 events. Not a flag | **One record's** determination | **Nothing** — the record is gated | Fail-closed for exactly one issue; every other candidate is unaffected | `WARN events-page-bound-reached issue=#<n>` | **AM-5**, unchanged |

**The sufficiency stop is not in this table, and that is deliberate.** `--max-candidates` (default 10, may only lower) ends the walk when the *answer is already complete enough to act on* — it is not a bound on cost or on the queried set, and it cannot coexist with the `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` condition, which requires `eligible == 0`. `bin/hos-cron` consumes five (`head -5`); ten is deliberate headroom **so that the stop is never the thing that decides which issue is built.** It reports `unevaluated:sufficient` and sets `complete=no`, because a walk that stopped early is a walk that stopped early regardless of how comfortable the reason was.

**On pagination — the ruling and its price (AF-8).** Step C fetched a single unpaginated page in revision 3, preserved as R-6 on the stated grounds that it *"preserves today's documented 100-issue single-page ceiling exactly."* **AF-8 shows that ceiling is three records from binding, and that it moves toward the oldest issues monotonically.** Measured by me this session (§0.0.3): **97 non-PR records on a page whose maximum is 100**, with #1539 at position 88 — the architect measured 97/position 88 the day before and the panel measured 96/position 86 earlier that same day. A `created desc` list moves old issues toward the truncation edge with every new filing, and #1539 is roughly a dozen filings from falling off the page entirely. **A P-1 fix that changed the cap and left the page would have fixed the instance and left the class** — the failure would return in weeks with an identical user-visible signature (*#1539 is authorized and never gets built*) but a different cause and an absent diagnostic. **Binding: Step C paginates, bounded at 5 pages / 500 records**, normalising concatenated page arrays with the `re.sub(r"\]\s*\[", ",", out)` helper §1.4 already specifies for events. The cost is **≤4 extra list requests per cycle** — negligible beside the 100 the ceiling already permits. **R-6 is closed (§7.2), not carried.**

**The division of labour between the three, stated so the next reader does not "simplify" one into another:** **pagination buys *ordering completeness*** — it is what lets a `priority:critical` record filed 150 issues ago be ranked at all; **the cost ceiling buys *cost containment***; **and the ranking is what makes the ceiling safe.** Dropping pagination re-opens AF-8. Dropping the ranking re-opens P-1. Raising the ceiling above the population buys nothing and costs rate limit. They are one design, in the same way AM-18's skip rule and AM-4's title derivation are one construction, and the same warning applies.

**Why the list bound exits 0 despite failing my own criterion — stated rather than smuggled.** It is enumerable but **unordered**, so strictly the criterion above pushes it toward exit 2. It exits 0 anyway, for one reason and with one obligation, both of which are the architect's (AM-21) and neither of which is mine to soften. **The reason:** exiting 2 there converts a large backlog into a **repo-wide stop** with no recovery act available to anyone — the denial-of-service argument revision 3's D5.1 deployed in the wrong place, and which is valid *here*. **The obligation:** because it is unordered truncation, its report is the loudest class, and the bound is set high enough that reaching it is an **operational emergency rather than a residual**. An architect who uses "it would stop the repo" to wave away an unordered truncation, and does not then make the truncation impossible in practice, has not ruled anything — which is why the bound is 500 and not 200.

**What this costs to run, measured rather than asserted.** Worst case per cycle after these rulings: **1–5 list requests + up to 100 events requests** ≈ **105 requests**. At the documented five-minute worker cadence that is ≈ **1,260 requests/hour against a 5,000/hour installation limit**. **Provenance, stated precisely because Amendment 3 §3(c) specifically warns against laundering a derived number as a measured one:** the **100-check half is a hard bound** (it is the ceiling's definition); the **"one events page per issue" half is DERIVED** from this repo's measured maximum of **17 events on any issue** — which is the architect's measurement, cited from Amendment 3 §0, **not re-derived by me**, and which is a property of a *young* repo that will decay. The **≈105 figure is therefore derived, not measured**, and the architect flags it in Amendment 3's own CONFIDENCE block as the claim H1 rests on that is least well grounded. It is handed to the panel as §9/**P1-b**.

**The lever, named so nobody looks for it in the wrong place.** This cost is **FR7's** price, not the ceiling's: *"no state file, no memo"* means every cycle re-derives every authorization from live events. **If the cost is ever judged unacceptable, the thing to revisit is FR7** — a `pm-agent`/human decision about whether an authorization may be memoised and under what invalidation rule — **not the ceiling**, which would buy the saving back in exactly the coin P-1 was paid in. **Any future proposal to lower the ceiling below the candidate population must cite this paragraph and state what it is trading.** The step from ≈25 to ≈100 paid calls per cycle is a change to the operating-cost envelope of the work-selection path, so it is **disclosed to the human at H1** (Amendment 3 §3(b) item 2's new cost bullet) rather than bound silently.

**Flags that deliberately do not exist (AD-13 — no knob may widen the trusted set or disable the gate):**

- **No `--repo-root`.** The repo root is derived from `Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2]`. A `--repo-root` flag would let a caller point the gate at a directory containing a forged `.github/CODEOWNERS` or a populated `trusted-requesters.txt` — a complete bypass through an ergonomics flag. The *library* functions in `requester_trust.py` remain `repo_root`-parameterised (probe.py and the tests need that); the *entry point* is not.
- **No `--exclude-label`.** The exclusion set is the module constant `EXCLUDED_LABELS = ("needs-human",)`. A flag could remove the `needs-human` exclusion. **This constant is now the bound landing site for `ADR-1604` AD-5's queue exclusions (AM-9)** — the architect amended AD-5's *mechanism* bullet so the exclusion is expressed **once, here**, with no inlined twin in `worker-cron-prompt.md` and no lock-step divergence test, because after S2 there is no second implementation to diverge from. AD-5's *semantics* are unchanged and still binding. Note the scope precisely, so #1604's author is not misled: AD-5 makes one narrow marker authoritative for **both** the stuck-count and the queue exclusion; only the **exclusion** half lands here. The count half stays in #1604's stuck-set query. See §4.5.
- **No `--trusted`, `--allow`, `--skip-gate`, `--dry-run-trust` or any equivalent.** A test enumerates the parser's actions and asserts the flag set is exactly the **five** above (`--repo`, `--milestone`, `--label`, `--max-candidates`, `--max-authorization-checks`). **The figure changed from four to five in revision 4** because AM-19 adds `--max-candidates`; the test's assertion changes with it, and a coder who finds a stale "four" anywhere in this document should treat it as a defect in the document, not in the parser.
- **No `--max-list-pages`.** The 5-page / 500-record list bound (§2.2.1) is a module constant, not a flag, for the same reason `EXCLUDED_LABELS` is: a flag that lowers it would let a caller re-create AF-8's truncation on demand, silently, in the one region of the order that is *unordered*. Lowering the **cost ceiling** is safe because its truncation is ranked; lowering the **page bound** is not, and the asymmetry is the whole content of §2.2.1's table.
- **`--label` is permitted** because it cannot widen trust: it changes only *which records are queried*, and every returned record still passes the identical trust and authorization tests. It exists so the `needs-ai` → `needs-worker` rename (#1349) is a one-line change rather than a sixth hardcoded literal (FR26). If the label is renamed and the default is not updated, the query returns nothing → zero candidates → fail-closed (AD-4's stated rename-survivability property).
- **`--max-authorization-checks` may only lower.** Effective value is `min(100, max(0, provided))`. **The ceiling changed from 25 to 100 in revision 4 and its *meaning* changed with it** (AM-19): it is a **cost ceiling**, not a coverage budget. It no longer decides *which* records are evaluated — the ranked walk decides that — only *how many* paid checks the cycle may spend. A reviewer who reads this as "the cap was raised because 25 was too small" has the ruling backwards: **the cap's value was never the defect.**
- **`--max-candidates` may only lower.** Effective value is `min(10, max(1, provided))` — note the lower clamp is **1**, not 0, because a sufficiency stop of zero would emit an empty list on every cycle while exiting 0 and reporting `complete=no`, which is a gate that does nothing while claiming to have worked. `--max-authorization-checks 0` remains legal and means "evaluate only the trusted fast path", which is a coherent (if useless) cost position; `--max-candidates 0` is not coherent and is rejected by the clamp.

**Environment.** The gate requires `gh` to be authenticated already (`GH_TOKEN` exported by the cron launcher, or the worker's ambient session auth). It **MUST NOT mint or revoke tokens** — putting credential handling on the selection path adds a failure mode and a secret-handling surface to a gate whose whole value is being boring and deterministic.

**GitHub access.** The gate shells out to `gh api` directly, with `subprocess.run`, following the precedent already set in the same directory by `require_human_approval.py:_fetch_reviews`. It does **not** import `scripts.automation.lib.github._run_gh`, which is functionally the right helper but sits on an unprotected surface (§1.1). Search performed before writing this: `scripts/`, `bootstrap/`, `bin/`, and `scripts/automation/lib/*.py` — found `scripts/automation/lib/github.py::_run_gh` (right behaviour, wrong trust direction), `bootstrap/query_issues.sh` (mints its own token per call, emits a summary line with no `.user` fields, so unusable here), and `require_human_approval.py::_fetch_reviews` (right directory, right pattern, review-specific). If a third `scripts/framework/**` consumer ever needs `gh api`, the helper should be promoted **into** `scripts/framework/`, never imported out of `scripts/automation/`.

### 2.3 Algorithm (ordered; the order is part of the contract)

**Step A — arguments.** `--repo` must match `^[A-Za-z0-9_.-]+/[A-Za-z0-9_.-]+$`. `--milestone` must parse as a positive integer. This second check is load-bearing: `bin/hos-cron` substitutes `@@MILESTONE_NUMBER@@` into the worker prompt **only when `HOS_TARGET_MILESTONE_NUMBER` is non-empty** (`bin/hos-cron:1808-1815`), so an unresolved milestone leaves the literal placeholder in the fallback command. `--milestone @@MILESTONE_NUMBER@@` must fail closed, not be coerced. Any violation → exit 2.

**Step B — configuration, all fail-closed.**

1. `bots = load_bot_accounts()` — empty → exit 2 `bot-accounts-empty`. (The house precedent: `require_human_approval.py` exits 2 on empty `BOT_ACCOUNTS`.)
2. `codeowners = codeowners_humans()` — empty → exit 2 `codeowners-empty`. An absent, unreadable, or team-patterns-only CODEOWNERS means **no actor can ever authorize anything**, so running is pointless and silence would be dangerous. Note this is stricter than the shared loader, which returns an empty set; the primitive stays permissive-of-absence for `probe.py` compatibility and the *gate* enforces.
3. `apps = load_trusted_apps()` — empty → exit 2 `trusted-apps-empty`.
4. `roster = load_trusted_requesters()` — absent → empty set, continue. A non-`FileNotFoundError` read error → exit 2 `roster-unreadable`.
5. Any other exception in Step B → exit 2 `config-error`.

**Step C — the list query, PAGINATED (changed in revision 4 — AM-22 / AF-8).**

```
gh api --paginate "repos/<repo>/issues?state=open&milestone=<n>&labels=<label>&per_page=100"
```

Bounded to **5 pages / 500 records** (§2.2.1), normalising `gh --paginate`'s concatenated page arrays with the `re.sub(r"\]\s*\[", ",", out)` idiom §1.4 specifies for events — the same helper, for the same reason, and **not** a naive `replace("][", ",")`, whose failure mode `require_human_approval.py`'s own comment records.

Non-zero exit, non-JSON output, or a non-list result → exit 2 `list-query-failed` (F12, unchanged).

**Exhausting the 5-page bound is NOT an error.** It is the unordered-truncation case of §2.2.1: the walk proceeds on the 500 records it has, and the gate emits `WARN candidate-list-truncated pages=5 records=500` — **the loudest report class** — and sets `complete=no` with `unevaluated:list-truncated` on the unevaluated line. **Reaching this bound is an operational emergency, not a residual.** Revision 3 preserved a single unpaginated page as R-6 on the grounds that it *"preserves today's documented 100-issue single-page ceiling exactly"*; **R-6 is now CLOSED (§7.2)**, because AF-8 established that the ceiling was three records from binding and moves toward the oldest issues with every new filing — and because the region it drops is exactly the region P-1 starved.

*Traceability (AR-5):* Step C implements **FR8** (the gate must make an unapproved untrusted issue ineligible **at every path that selects work** — this is the only path after §4.1's deletion) and **FR26** (the `--label` flag is what makes the `needs-ai` → `needs-worker` rename a one-line change; AR-6 marks FR26 *structurally yes* for S2).

**Step D — per record. Free filters first, then RANK, then pay (restructured in revision 4 — AM-19).**

> **The one-sentence statement of the ruling, carried here in the architect's terms because the sentence *is* the design:** *rank every candidate before spending a single authorization check, walk the ranked list, stop when enough eligible candidates have been found or when the cost ceiling is reached, and never admit a candidate found after the stop — so that whatever the gate emits is always an exact prefix of the answer a complete walk would have produced.*

| Step | Rule | Cost | Implements (AR-5) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **D1** | `record.get("pull_request")` present → **skip** | free | DEV-1, §7.1; **approved by AM-12 subject to §6's binding added test.** Scope boundary of **AD-14** — the *issue* gate must not adjudicate PR eligibility |
| **D2** | any label name in `EXCLUDED_LABELS` → skip | free | The one landing site (AM-9 / §2.2); `ADR-1604` **AD-5**'s exclusion half |
| **D3** | `verdict = requester_verdict(record, trusted_set)` — the **trust** determination | free | **FR1, FR2, FR4 (as amended by `REQUIREMENTS-1540-AMENDMENT-1` AR-3), FR5.** FR4-as-amended governs *here* and only here: trust is a function of the issue **author** as reported by the API, and no metadata act by any actor confers it |
| **D4** | `verdict.trusted` → **eligible**, `authorization = "requester-trusted:" + verdict.reason`, **zero extra API calls** | free | **FR1, FR2, FR3** (roster), **AD-2** (the marker narrowing) |
| **D-rank** | **NEW, and it precedes D5.** Rank the **whole** candidate set: `priority:critical`→0, `priority:high`→1, `priority:medium`→2, else 3. Order by `(rank, number)` ascending — **the identical total order Step E already sorts by** | free | **AM-19.** Ranking is free: `priority:*` is a label, labels are in the Step C payload, and the rank function reads nothing else |
| **D5** | Walk the ranked list. An untrusted record costs **one** authorization check — the **authorization** determination (below) | **paid** | **FR7** (never cached), **FR8**, **FR16**, **FR28.** Per AR-4, **D5 does not narrow** under the FR4 ruling: it is the mechanism #1539's 2026-09-10 human ruling item 1 names, word for word |

**Why the cap's *value* was never the defect, and why D6 became D-rank.** Revision 3 spent a paid operation (D5) before a free ordering operation (D6). That is the error, and it is visible only once the two are put side by side. Measured against the live queue by me this session: **3 `priority:critical` records among 97** (#1678, #1643, #1539), and the walk's key is `(rank, number)` ascending — so under a ranked walk **#1539 is the FIRST record evaluated**, and third at absolute worst if the tie-break within a rank were reversed. Inside a cap of 25, inside a cap of 5, inside any cap a cost model would plausibly choose. **It was evaluated at position 88 of 97 under revision 3 as written.** The panel's "not fixable by raising the cap" is correct, and its converse is the interesting half: **once the order is right, the cap almost stops mattering** — which is the strongest available evidence that order, not budget, was the defect.

**Why ranking alone is not enough.** With a ceiling in rank order, the gate evaluates the criticals first and then works down. If every evaluated record is gated and the human has authorized one beyond the ceiling, the gate reports `eligible=0`, emits `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED`, and the authorized issue is never built. **That is P-1 moved, not removed** — a smaller region, the same permanent unselectability. So the ceiling must not bind in normal operation (§2.2.1, and §8/E-8 on the one case where that requirement is not yet satisfied), and when it does bind, what it drops must be the lowest-ranked records **and it must say so** (AM-20).

**D5 — live actor-derived authorization.** For each untrusted-authored record, **in rank order**:

1. **The two stops, and what they do (replaced in revision 4 — AM-19 replaces revision 3's D5.1 entirely).**

   - **Sufficiency stop.** When the eligible set reaches `--max-candidates` (default 10), **stop the walk.** Report `unevaluated:sufficient`.
   - **Cost ceiling.** `auth_checks += 1` before each paid check. If `auth_checks > effective_ceiling` (default 100), **stop the walk.** Emit `WARN authorization-cost-ceiling-reached evaluated=<E> unevaluated=<U>` and report `unevaluated:cost-ceiling`. Hitting the ceiling means the gate's answer is bounded by **budget** rather than by the **queue** — an operational condition, not a detail, which is why it gets its own WARN and not merely a field (AM-20 point 5).
   - **On either stop, the walk ends COMPLETELY. No further record is admitted — trusted or otherwise.** This is the part revision 3 got wrong in a way that only a ranked walk exposes: revision 3's D5.1 skipped *"this and every remaining **untrusted** record"* and kept admitting trusted ones. Under a ranked walk that would emit a trusted record at rank position 90 while an authorized record at position 30 was never evaluated — **precisely the "silently demote a `priority:critical` item" hazard D5.3 refuses to accept**, arrived at through the front door.
   - **Both stops exit 0 and report.** Neither is an error. Exiting 2 on a ceiling hit would mean that anyone who can get N untrusted-authored issues into the milestone denies the worker *all* work, including trusted work — turning a cost control into a denial-of-service. But that argument is only valid because the omission is now **enumerable and ordered**; in revision 3 it was deployed to excuse an omission that was neither. See §2.2.1's criterion.

   > **The property that makes a partial answer safe, stated precisely — because §3 row F15 got it wrong in a way worth naming, and because this sentence is the whole of P-1.**
   >
   > F15 accepted the cap as safe because *"a partial answer is a subset of the eligible set."* **A subset is NOT sufficient; a PREFIX is.** Under AM-19 every early stop truncates a totally-ordered walk, so the emitted list is byte-identical to the first *k* lines a complete walk would have emitted, and **nothing omitted can outrank anything emitted.** Under the cap-in-API-order it was an arbitrary subset of an *unrelated* order, so the emitted list was not a prefix of anything and a `priority:critical` record could sit — **did sit** — entirely outside it.
   >
   > Revision 3's §9/T1 invited the panel to *"find the exception"* to the reasoning that a subset is always safe. **The exception is that it is not a subset property at all; it is an ordering property.** A design that reasons about subsets when the hazard is about order will keep producing this defect in new places, which is why this is stated as a property of the algorithm rather than as a fix to a flag.

   **Prefix-correctness, as a checkable claim rather than an assertion**, because §9 hands exactly this to the panel: *for any stop point k, the emitted list equals the first k elements of `sorted(all_eligible, key=(rank, number))`.* It holds if and only if four things hold together — (a) the rank function reads only the Step C payload, so a record's rank cannot change between D-rank and emit; (b) `(rank, number)` is a **total** order on the candidate set, so there are no ties to break non-deterministically (issue numbers are unique); (c) D1/D2's free filters are applied **before** ranking, so the ranked set and the eligible-candidate universe are drawn from the same population; and (d) no record is admitted after a stop. **Each of those four is a separate test in §6.2, not one test named `test_prefix_correctness`** — a single test over a conjunction reports "prefix-correctness broke" and leaves the next reader to find out which limb.

2. `record["milestone"]["title"]` absent → **skip the record, and emit `WARN milestone-object-absent issue=#<n> (milestone-scoped response contained a record with no milestone object)` to stderr** (AM-18, binding — the full diagnostic contract is §1.6's table and applies identically here). Never authorize blind, and never fall back to the labeled channel. **This branch is unreachable while the invariant holds and that is the point:** Step C queries `&milestone=<n>` server-side, so every record carries a milestone object by construction; one that does not means the *response* is not the shape this design assumes, and the only response that does not act on a premise known to be false is to refuse the candidate. It is also inseparable from D5.4's derive-the-title-from-the-record construction — the title is taken from the record precisely because the server-side filter guarantees it is the milestone in question, and that guarantee is what has just failed here. The diagnostic exists so the branch is **measurably** unreachable rather than assumed so. **A skip here is a determination** (the gate looked and refused), so it counts toward `gated`, not `unevaluated`.
2. `record["milestone"]["title"]` absent → **skip the record, and emit `WARN milestone-object-absent issue=#<n> (milestone-scoped response contained a record with no milestone object)` to stderr** (AM-18, binding — the full diagnostic contract is §1.6's table and applies identically here). Never authorize blind, and never fall back to the labeled channel. **This branch is unreachable while the invariant holds and that is the point:** Step C queries `&milestone=<n>` server-side, so every record carries a milestone object by construction; one that does not means the *response* is not the shape this design assumes, and the only response that does not act on a premise known to be false is to refuse the candidate. It is also inseparable from D5.4's derive-the-title-from-the-record construction — the title is taken from the record precisely because the server-side filter guarantees it is the milestone in question, and that guarantee is what has just failed here. The diagnostic exists so the branch is **measurably** unreachable rather than assumed so.
3. Fetch `gh api --paginate "repos/<repo>/issues/<n>/events?per_page=100"`, bounded to 10 pages / 1000 events, normalising concatenated page arrays per §1.4. **Transport error, non-zero exit, or unparseable output → exit 2 `events-query-failed:<n>`.**

   **This rule is UNCHANGED by revision 4 and AM-21 confirms F13 exactly as written — but its reasoning is now the general rule rather than a local argument, and the change of status is worth noticing.** The failure lands on an **arbitrary** record mid-walk. The gate cannot say whether that record would have ranked first, and continuing would emit a list that is not a prefix of anything. **Neither of §2.2.1's two properties holds:** the omission is not enumerable (the gate does not know what it would have found) and it is not ordered (the failure fell where the transport failed, not where the order put it). So the determination is **unknown in extent**, and the gate MUST exit 2.

   **Read that against the two stops above, because the contrast is the whole of AM-21.** A cost-ceiling stop and an events-query failure both leave records unevaluated. One exits 0 and one exits 2, and revision 3 had no principle that distinguished them — it simply argued each locally and, predictably, argued them in opposite directions. The distinguishing property is not "how many records" or "how bad it is": it is whether the gate can **state the extent** of what it missed and **place it at the tail of an order it controls.**

   **Exhausting the page bound without a match is *not* an error**; it is "not authorized" → skip that candidate, **and it MUST emit `WARN events-page-bound-reached issue=#<n>` to stderr** (AM-5). That is a **determination** about one record (it counts toward `gated`), it truncates nothing in the walk's order, and every other candidate is unaffected. A stall that nobody can see is R-5 becoming permanent.
4. `actor = verify_codeowner_actor(events, codeowners, bots, label, expected_milestone_title=record["milestone"]["title"])` — a non-`None` title always, per AM-4. Passing `None` here would silently disable the milestone channel, which is safe but wrong: the gate holds the title and has no excuse not to bind it.
5. `actor is None` → skip. Otherwise **eligible**, `authorization = "codeowner-actor:" + actor`.

**Step E — ordering (preserved exactly, and now redundant by construction).** `sorted(eligible, key=lambda c: (c.rank, c.number))`. Emit `#{number} [{priority}] {title}` with `title` defaulting to `""` and **no truncation**. Python's `sorted` and jq's `sort_by` are both stable and the key is total, so the two are equivalent by construction, not by luck. Degenerate records (absent or `null` `labels`) must not raise — they rank `low` and stay eligible, matching the jq filter's `(.labels // [])` and its explicit test.

**New in revision 4: Step E's sort is now a no-op on a correct implementation, and it MUST be retained anyway.** D-rank walks in `(rank, number)` order and the eligible set is accumulated in walk order, so E re-sorts an already-sorted list. **Retain it**: it is the single line that pins the output contract independently of the walk, it costs nothing, and if a future edit breaks D-rank's ordering the output stays correct while §6.2's prefix tests fail — which is the right failure, in the right place, with the right diagnosis. **Do not "optimise" it away on the observation that it is redundant.** Its redundancy is the point: it is the second of two independent statements of the same order, and Amendment 3's risk register names *"the ranking being optimised back below the authorization check"* as the single edit that restores P-1 with every order-agnostic test still green.

**Step F — the stderr summary, the completeness contract, and AM-13's held-everything line (binding; substantially rewritten in revision 4 — AM-20, AM-24).**

Always exactly one summary line. **Four fields are new:**

```
select_work_candidates: repo=<slug> milestone=<n> scanned=<N> evaluated=<E> eligible=<M> \
    authorized=<K> gated=<G> unevaluated=<U> auth_checks=<A> complete=<yes|no>
```

| Field | Meaning | New? |
|---|---|---|
| `scanned=<N>` | Records returned by Step C after D1/D2's free filters — the candidate **set** | no |
| `evaluated=<E>` | Records the walk reached a **determination** on: eligible or gated | **yes (AM-20)** |
| `eligible=<M>` | Candidates emitted on stdout | no |
| `authorized=<K>` | Of the eligible, how many are eligible **via D5** — a verified human CODEOWNER's event — as distinct from eligible via **D4** (trusted authorship). `K ≤ M` | **yes (AM-24)** |
| `gated=<G>` | Records the gate **determined** are held. **Determinations only** | semantics changed |
| `unevaluated=<U>` | Records the walk **never reached**. `N = E + U` and `E = M + G` | **yes (AM-20)** |
| `auth_checks=<A>` | Paid authorization checks spent this cycle | no |
| `complete=<yes\|no>` | `yes` iff `U == 0` and no bound truncated the candidate set | **yes (AM-20)** |

**`complete=yes` is an invariant, not a field.** It asserts that the gate looked at every record the query returned and that the query returned every record there is. Emitting it when either is false is the single failure this pair of rulings exists to make impossible, and §6.2 tests it as an invariant — every early-stop and every truncation path asserts `complete=no`, and the complete path asserts `complete=yes` — rather than as one happy-path assertion.

**When `U > 0`, one additional line, always** — separate from the `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` block and emitted whether or not that block fires:

```
select_work_candidates: UNEVALUATED <U> of <N> records were not evaluated: \
    unevaluated:sufficient=<x> unevaluated:cost-ceiling=<y> unevaluated:list-truncated=<z>
```

**And, when `eligible == 0 and gated > 0`, the distinct, unmissable held-everything block** (AM-13 point 2). This is not the same fact as the summary and must not be left for an operator to infer by comparing two numbers at 3am:

```
select_work_candidates: ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED <G> of <N> in-milestone issues are held for human authorization
select_work_candidates: ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED reasons: trusted-app-no-machine-filing-marker=<a> not-in-trusted-set=<b> no-codeowner-actor=<c> …
select_work_candidates: ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED issues: #1539 #1340 #1356 #1540 (+<k> more)
select_work_candidates: ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED INCOMPLETE — <U> further records were not evaluated (see UNEVALUATED above); this count is a FLOOR
```

**The fourth line is new in revision 4 and is emitted only when `complete=no`** (AM-20 point 4). Without it, a line reading *"<G> of <N> in-milestone issues are held for human authorization"* when *<U>* of them were never looked at is **a false statement in the one place the design put its trust.** AM-13 exists because a gate that holds everything and whispers is indistinguishable from an empty backlog; P-1 added a **third** state the design had no vocabulary for — *a gate that did not finish looking* — and three states, two of which are silent, is worse than the two-state problem AM-13 was pulled forward from S6 to fix.

**One interaction that falls out of the rulings and is worth stating because it removes a whole class of worry (AM-21).** The **sufficiency stop cannot coexist with the `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` condition**: the stop requires `eligible ≥ --max-candidates ≥ 1`, and the block requires `eligible == 0`. So **the one place AM-13's counts are load-bearing is always a complete-or-ceiling-or-truncated walk, never a sufficiency-truncated one.** The routine, expected, every-cycle early stop can never be the thing that makes the held-everything count wrong.

**Per-candidate authorization lines (AM-24, binding — new in revision 4).** For each candidate eligible **via D5**, the gate MUST emit one stderr line naming the issue number and the authorizing actor:

```
select_work_candidates: AUTHORIZED issue=#<n> actor=<login> via=<labeled|milestoned>
```

**State the limit before the feature, because this does NOT satisfy FR17 and must not be read as doing so.** `pm-agent` asked the architect whether any mechanism satisfies FR17 — *"approving without an assessment MUST NOT be silently possible"* — in the S2 era. **AM-24's answer is no, and the reason is structural rather than an absence of effort:** (i) the condition FR17 wants a signal about is **universal** in S2, because no assessor exists until S5, so every S2 authorization is an approval without an assessment — and a warning that fires on 100% of acts is not a warning, it is a banner, invisible by the second week; (ii) **S2 has no interception point** — the authorizing act is a human applying a label in the GitHub UI, and nothing in S2 sits between the act and its effect; the gate reads the resulting event minutes or days later; (iii) the one adjacent channel that could refuse at the moment of the act is `/approve`, which AF-5 establishes cannot authorize at all and which AM-25 now makes inert. **FR17 is deliberately deferred to S5** (`REQUIREMENTS-1540-AMENDMENT-1` AR-6), it is a **deferral rather than an omission** from the moment pm-agent wrote it down, and **Q4 must be answered before S5 binds it.**

What these lines *are*: in the S2 era they are **the entire machine-readable record that a human's unaided judgement was the only judgement in the loop.** AD-12's durable per-decision audit is S6, and S6 is a long way from a repo that is building work today. This is deliberately small — one field and one line per authorized candidate, on a stream DEV-2 already un-silences — and it is the S2-era half of what AD-12 will later do properly.

*Traceability (AR-5):* Step F implements **FR27** (AR-6: *Partly* — this is the S2 half; AD-11's full held-request listing is S6) and is the operator-surface half of **FR29**'s legibility requirement. It does **not** implement FR17, per the paragraph above.

Four binding properties of the held-everything block, unchanged in substance from revision 3:

1. **It must be distinguishable at a glance from "the milestone is empty."** `eligible=0 gated=0` and `eligible=0 gated=12` are opposite operational situations — nothing to do versus everything blocked on one person — and the summary line alone renders them as near-identical strings. The `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` token exists to be greppable and to be *visually* different, not to be parsed.
2. **It names the reason tokens and their counts** (AM-13's requirement), drawn from §1.2's closed reason-token set. `no-codeowner-actor` is the D5.5 outcome and is a gate-local token, not a `RequesterVerdict.reason`. **Binding, new in revision 4 (AM-20 point 3): the three `unevaluated:*` tokens MUST NOT appear in this breakdown.** It enumerates **determinations** only. They are reported on the separate `UNEVALUATED` line above, and mixing them here would restore exactly the conflation AM-20 exists to remove — a starved record counted as *"held for human authorization"*, which is a false claim about a person's queue.
3. **It names the issue numbers**, bounded — first 20, then `(+k more)`. AM-13 requires only the count and the reasons; I am adding the numbers because §2.7.3's cutover is not performable without them, and because the gate has already scanned at most 100 records so the information is free. This is deliberately **not** AD-11's full held-request listing mode, which stays in S6: no bodies, no titles, no per-issue explanation, one bounded line.
4. **It is never routed into an issue** (AM-13 point 3). A per-cycle issue-filing loop on this condition would be its own denial-of-wallet, and bounded-retry/escalation is AD-10's design, which belongs with the assessor slice. stderr and the cycle log, nothing more.

Durable per-decision audit events are AD-12/S6 and are **not** built here. These lines are the whole of the S2-era operator signal, which is why §2.4's removal of `2>/dev/null` is **required, not optional** (AM-13 point 1).

### 2.4 Call site 1 — `bin/hos-cron` (the pre-computed candidates block)

**Today** (`:1131-1146`): the actionable-work gate runs `gh api …` piped through `--jq "$(cat "$REPO_ROOT/scripts/automation/lib/next_candidates.jq")"` with `2>/dev/null`, capturing into `_GATE_CANDIDATES` and setting `_gate_candidates_ok`. `_build_context` (`:1886-1897`) reuses `_GATE_CANDIDATES` and `head -5`s it into the `### Next work candidates` section.

**After:**

```
  _GATE_CANDIDATES=""
  _gate_candidates_ok=1
  _gate_err="$(mktemp)"
  if [[ -n "${HOS_TARGET_MILESTONE_NUMBER:-}" ]]; then
    if _GATE_CANDIDATES=$( (cd "$REPO_ROOT" && python3 -m scripts.framework.select_work_candidates \
        --repo "$_REPO_SLUG" --milestone "$HOS_TARGET_MILESTONE_NUMBER") 2>"$_gate_err" ); then
      _gate_candidates_ok=1
    else
      _gate_candidates_ok=0
      _GATE_CANDIDATES=""
    fi
    cat "$_gate_err" >&2                                    # DEV-2: nothing is swallowed
    if grep -q 'ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED' "$_gate_err"; then    # AM-13 point 3
      echo "$LOG_PREFIX ALL WORK CANDIDATES GATED — every in-milestone issue is held for human authorization; this is NOT an empty backlog (#1539/#1540, see the select_work_candidates lines above)"
    fi
  fi
  rm -f "$_gate_err"
```

Unchanged around it: `_gate_all_ok` composition (`:1152-1156`), the `no actionable work` skip (`:1158-1163`), `_build_context`'s consumption and `head -5` (`:1894-1895`). The `(cd "$REPO_ROOT" && …)` subshell mirrors the established `_audit` invocation at `:364`.

**Deliberate change, DEV-2 — required, not optional (AM-13 point 1):** the `2>/dev/null` at `:1139` is **removed**. Nothing about control flow changes; a silent skip is precisely the failure mode `CLAUDE.md` names as the one to guard against, and a fail-closed gate that says nothing is indistinguishable from an empty backlog.

**Why DEV-2 alone does not satisfy AM-13 point 3, and why the temp file is there.** I re-read `bin/hos-cron:113-114` this revision: the documented crontab is `>> /tmp/hos-worker-hos.log 2>&1`, so with `2>/dev/null` gone the gate's stderr does reach the cycle log in the shipped configuration. That is necessary and not sufficient, for two reasons the amendment's point 3 exists to cover. First, it is **configuration-dependent** — a consumer who redirects only stdout loses the entire signal, and AM-13's whole point is that this condition must not be losable. Second, raw subprocess stderr arrives **without `$LOG_PREFIX`**, so it is invisible to every existing habit and tool that greps the log by `[hos-worker-cron:<project>]`; the one line an operator most needs would be the one line that does not match their filter. Capturing stderr, re-emitting it verbatim, and then adding **one** prefixed line on the `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` token fixes both without parsing the gate's output for anything load-bearing — the token is a presence check, never a value the control flow depends on.

**What `bin/hos-cron` must NOT do with it:** not suppress the raw stderr in favour of the prefixed line (the reason tokens and issue numbers live in the raw lines); not branch on it (`_gate_candidates_ok` and `_GATE_CANDIDATES` remain the only control-flow inputs — a gated-everything cycle is *exit 0 with an empty list*, behaviourally identical to an empty milestone, and deliberately so); and **not file an issue about it** (AM-13 point 3).

**Semantics after the change — trace it through, because this is where AD-3's "the fallback is the same gated script" pays off:**

| Gate result | `_gate_candidates_ok` | Context section | Worker behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|
| exit 0, candidates | 1 | the list | picks the first non-blocked candidate |
| exit 0, empty | 1 | `(none available or fetch failed)` | Step 2 fallback → **same gated command** → same empty result → no work |
| exit 2 (any cause) | 0 | `(none available or fetch failed)` | Step 2 fallback → **same gated command** → exit 2 → no work |

In the third row `_gate_all_ok=0` means the cycle does **not** take the `#1395` early skip — Step 0 triage, Step 0.5 release gating and Step 1 PR handling all still run. Only *new work selection* is closed. That is the intended split: the gate closes work selection, not the cycle.

**One property worth stating because a reviewer will ask:** this block runs *before* the cycle's git sync (`bin/hos-cron:1004-1017` explains why), so the gate code executed is the local clone's, one sync behind `origin/main`. That is unchanged from today, where `cat next_candidates.jq` read the same local tree.

### 2.5 Call site 2 — `bootstrap/worker-cron-prompt.md` Step 2

**Today** (`:97-101`): a `gh api … --jq "$(cat scripts/automation/lib/next_candidates.jq)"` fallback. That command is **unallowlistable under `CLAUDE.md`** (command substitution). **What happens to it on an unattended cycle is UNRESOLVED, and revision 4 stops asserting an answer (AM-28(g), binding).**

> **The discrepancy, stated rather than resolved.** Revision 3 asserted that on an unattended cycle the command *"is denied outright and silently skipped"*, citing `CLAUDE.md`'s sandbox section. **`bin/hos-cron:1764` launches `("$CLAUDE_BIN" --print --permission-mode bypassPermissions)`** — read by me this session — with `:1758-1762` stating in terms that this *"clears the approval gate so the agent can run gh/git/bash unattended"* and that the safety net is the App's scoped permissions, **"NOT the CLI permission gate."** The two repo sources describe opposite outcomes for the same command in the same situation. **I adopt neither side.** I have not run the experiment that would settle it, and an argument that rests on an unreconciled premise is an argument that changes its mind when someone reads the other file.
>
> **No design consequence either way** — exactly as the panel says. AD-3's collapse of the two call sites does not depend on which reading is right: under the `CLAUDE.md` reading the current fallback is silently skipped and the gate is never reached; under the `bypassPermissions` reading the current fallback *runs*, un-gated, and reopens the bypass S2 exists to close. **Both readings are reasons to replace it, and they are the same reason at different severities.** The replacement is required either way.
>
> **The contradiction itself is load-bearing well outside this design, and is recommended as a follow-up issue rather than carried here** (Amendment 3 §4 item 4). *"The agent cannot run that unattended"* is a premise this repo uses in several places; if `bypassPermissions` means it can, those arguments need re-reading. **It is not S2's to carry**, and S2 must not acquire a seventh thing to carry.

**After** — the fallback becomes one static command with no substitution, no inline `jq`, and no variable expansion (`@@MILESTONE_NUMBER@@` is substituted by `_build_prompt` before the LLM ever sees it, so the rendered text is fully literal):

```
python3 -m scripts.framework.select_work_candidates --repo thurlow-research/HumanOversightSystem --milestone @@MILESTONE_NUMBER@@
```

**The prose that must accompany it is part of the contract, not decoration:**

> This command is the **only** sanctioned way to produce work candidates. If it exits non-zero it has **failed closed** — there are no candidates this cycle. Do NOT construct your own query, do NOT fall back to `gh api`, do NOT read the issue list by any other means, and do NOT pick an issue from the Step 0 triage list. STOP after Steps 0/0.5/1.

Without that paragraph a capable model will helpfully improvise a `gh api` query and reopen the bypass in the most sympathetic possible way. A conformance test (§6.3) asserts the prompt contains this instruction and contains **no** `gh api …labels=needs-ai…` query and **no** `--jq`.

**AM-11 — the binding acceptance condition, because G11 is superseded rather than merely overlapped.** `ADR-1542` §4.9 (G11) planned a sandbox-allowlistable wrapper around `next_candidates.jq` for exactly this call site. S2's entry point **is** that wrapper, built for a different reason, and building G11 separately would produce the duplicate-gate defect (#1135) in its purest form: two wrappers over the same selection decision, one of them trust-gated. The architect has therefore marked G11 superseded and orphaned its sign-offs **for that item only** — with a condition attached that this document must meet, not merely intend:

> The rendered fallback invocation MUST contain **no command substitution, no variable expansion, no inline `jq`, and no backslash continuation.**

That is G11's actual requirement. **The justification for accepting it is restated in revision 4 on a ground that holds either way (AM-28(g), binding), because revision 3's rested on the premise §2.5's opening now records as unresolved.**

> **The fallback MUST be sandbox-allowlistable because `CLAUDE.md` requires it of every command in this repo** — a standing, documented rule, independent of what any particular runtime does with a non-conforming command. That is the whole justification, and it is the same justification whether an unallowlistable command is silently skipped or silently *executed with the permission gate bypassed*. Revision 3 instead argued that a non-conforming command *"would fail to execute"*, which is one of the two readings and is the weaker argument in the worse way: **if the `bypassPermissions` reading is the right one, revision 3's argument is not merely unproven — it is backwards**, because the failure it invokes as the reason to fix the line is the failure that would *not* occur, and the command would run un-gated instead.

The command above satisfies all four conditions by construction, because `@@MILESTONE_NUMBER@@` is substituted by `_build_prompt` *before* the LLM ever sees the text, so the rendered string is wholly literal. **§6.3's conformance test is the mechanism that keeps it true, and AM-11 makes it required, not optional** — an intention about a prompt string, unpinned by a test, survives exactly until the next person needs a second milestone. **§6.3 also now specifies *how* that test obtains the rendered string** (AM-28(a)), which revision 3 left unstated: an unspecified mechanism in a test that pins a security-relevant string is a test that gets written the easy way.

**Note on AD-7, binding:** this design assumes Step 0 does the wrong thing. Nothing above depends on Step 0's routing behaviour. S4 may improve Step 0; the gate's correctness does not move when it does.

### 2.6 Worked trace of the five live candidates

Applying §2.3 to the five candidates the dispatching worker verified (author, then `needs-ai`/milestone actors). Revision 1 labelled this "before §8/E-1 is ruled"; **E-1 is now ruled (AM-1) and the trace is unchanged** — option C keeps AD-2 exactly as written, so every verdict below stands. What changed is the *interpretation*: this is no longer a provisional picture pending a widening, it is the intended steady state.

| Issue | Author | `needs-ai` / milestone actor | D3 verdict | D5 result | Selectable? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| #1539 | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | untrusted — `trusted-app-no-machine-filing-marker` | actor is a bot → `is_bot_reviewer` → `continue` → `None` | **No** |
| #1340 | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | same | same | **No** |
| #1356 | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | same | same | **No** |
| #1540 | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | same | same | **No** |
| #1643 | `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` | `ScottThurlow` (needs-ai), `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` (milestone) | untrusted | `labeled` event actor `ScottThurlow` — not a bot, in CODEOWNERS → **authorized** | **Yes** |
| #1678 | `hos-worker-hos[bot]` | `hos-worker-hos[bot]` (both) | untrusted — title has no marker | bot actor → `continue` → `None` | **No** — this is the self-authorization shape the gate exists to reject |

This is the correct, intended behaviour of every line of §1–§2 as written. It is also a near-dead work loop, which is why E-1 was an escalation and not a footnote. The mechanism has a *working* release valve — #1643 passes purely because a human clicked `needs-ai` from their own account, which is precisely AM-1's option C in action, observed rather than theorised.

**Correction, AM-6 / AF-5 — revision 1 got the valve's shape wrong in one clause.** Revision 1's §8/E-1 option C said an issue becomes selectable when `ScottThurlow` personally applies `needs-ai` or the milestone from their own account **"or comments `/approve`."** *That last clause is false.* `.github/workflows/label-swap.yml` performs the `/approve` label edit with `GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}` (`:36`), so the resulting `labeled` event's actor is `github-actions[bot]` — `type == "Bot"`, login ending `[bot]` — which `is_bot_reviewer` excludes unconditionally at layers 1 and 2, whether or not it appears in `BOT_ACCOUNTS`. `verify_codeowner_actor` walks events only. **A CODEOWNER commenting `/approve` therefore produces no authorization at all in S2.** The issue flips labels, the workflow posts `"✅ /approve — needs-human → needs-ai (authorized by @ScottThurlow)."`, and the gate still refuses it: fail-closed and therefore safe, and **a silent no-op that looks exactly like success** — the same failure mode AD-6 already condemns in that workflow's own `2>/dev/null || true`.

The first two clauses of option C are correct, and #1643 is the live proof of the first. Everything that follows from the correction — what the human must actually do, what the repo must stop telling them, and the deadlock this creates for the cutover set — is §2.7.

**Reading the `needs-ai` column above with AF-5 in mind:** the trace's actors are the *recorded event actors*, so an issue whose `needs-ai` arrived via `/approve` would show `github-actions[bot]` there, not the commenter. None of the six does, so no row changes. A reviewer checking this against live GitHub should expect `github-actions[bot]` on any issue whose label came from a slash command, and must not read the workflow's confirmation comment as evidence of who applied the label.

---

## 2.7 The S2-era authorization act (AM-1, AM-3, AM-6) — what the human does, what the repo must stop saying, and the one thing that does not work

This section is new in revision 2. It exists because AM-1 makes one demand of this document above all others: *a gate whose documented approval path silently does nothing is worse than no gate.* Everything here is about making the documented path the one that actually works.

### 2.7.1 The authorizing act — stated verbatim, as the amendment requires

> **The authorizing act must be performed by the human's own GitHub account.** `bootstrap/edit_issue.sh --app human` writes as `scottthurlow-claude[bot]`, which `is_bot_reviewer` excludes, so any tooling or documentation offering `edit_issue.sh --app human` as the approval path is wrong and must not be written. The valid S2-era acts are: **the GitHub web UI, the GitHub mobile app, or a personal-access-token `gh` invocation under `ScottThurlow`.**

Three binding consequences for the S2 change set:

1. **No approval helper may be written.** Not a script, not a `Makefile` target, not a documented one-liner, not a convenience wrapper "that just calls `edit_issue.sh` for you." Any such tool would run under an App identity and would produce a `labeled` event the gate excludes — a tool that appears to authorize and does not is strictly worse than no tool, because it converts a visible gap into an invisible one. This is the same reasoning as D-5: the failure is silent and looks like success.
2. **The sentence above must appear in whatever the human is asked to do at cutover** (§2.7.3), not only here.
3. **Nothing in S1/S2 may special-case the human-proxy App**, in either direction. It is one of the three `machine-accounts.env` App identities in AD-1(b), narrowed by AD-2 exactly like the other two. A carve-out written to make the queue move would be option A wearing a configuration costume.

Option **D** — the human-proxy session stops self-applying the dispatch label to its own filings, and ends every filing by telling the human the exact act required — is adopted as the ergonomic wrapper and is *also* the correct hygiene fix independent of ergonomics: a human-proxy session applying `needs-ai` to its own filing is the same self-authorization shape as the worker's Step 0 (VF-3), one identity removed. It should stop regardless. The `CLAUDE.md` human-proxy-section edit that needs is a protected-surface change, human-gated at merge, and is **not pre-authorized here** (AD-14). It is also **not** in S2's file list (§2.1) — it is a behaviour change to an interactive session, separable from the gate, and folding it into the critical-path fix would add a sixth protected surface for an ergonomic gain. **AM-17 confirms this reasoning and rules that the count is not six**, while requiring that the human be *told the surface exists* as a deliberately-excluded planned follow-up (Amendment 2 §3(b) item 4's closing paragraph) — recording an excluded surface is the difference between a scope boundary and an omission. If the human wants option D inside S2, the count is six and that is their call to make, not this document's.

### 2.7.2 `/approve` in the S2 era: it is made INERT (revision 4 — AM-25 supersedes revision 3's disposition)

> **Section heading changed in revision 4.** Through revision 3 this section was titled *"…and why it is not made to refuse"*, and §9/R5 handed that judgement call to the panel with its three stated reasons attached. **The panel falsified one reason; the architect checked the other two and falsified a second. AM-25 OVERRULES R5 and the disposition goes with its basis.** The reasoning is preserved below rather than deleted, because *how* a stated justification came to be wrong is the useful part.

**What `/approve` does in S2: NOTHING to any label.** Binding, and it is small in code and large in consequence:

> **In S2, `/approve`'s branch in `.github/workflows/label-swap.yml` performs NO label write.** It posts one comment stating that `/approve` does not authorize autonomous work and naming the act that does (§2.7.4's verbatim wording, from the human's own account), and it **changes no labels**. The `gh issue edit --remove-label needs-human --add-label needs-ai` at `:74` is **removed**, and the `2>/dev/null || true` that guards it goes with it. **`/decline` is untouched** (`:79`-`:82`, re-read by me this session — revision 3 cited it at `:83`, which was wrong).

**Three harms disappear at once, none of which wording could have reached:**

1. **The false *"authorized by @you"* claim** — AM-6 point 3's shipping condition, now satisfied by the command no longer doing anything to claim.
2. **The consumption of the human's one additive act** on an issue not yet carrying the dispatch label — AM-15's "actively harmful" finding, which revision 3 could only document, not fix.
3. **P-5's double invisibility.** The panel found that post-S2 `/approve` removes `needs-human` **as well as** adding `needs-ai`, so the issue leaves the human's queue while remaining unactionable by the worker — **invisible to both.** With no label write, `needs-human` is no longer stripped and the issue stays in the queue the human watches. **P-5 dissolves at the root rather than being mitigated by wording.**

**Why R5's basis is gone — the three stated reasons, checked (AM-25).** Revision 3 declined to make `/approve` refuse on three reasons, and recorded them explicitly. That record is the only reason this was checkable at all, and it is worth saying so before the table: a judgement call that states its reasons can be falsified; one that says "on balance" cannot.

| R5's stated reason | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| *"the runbook's intervention procedure"* references `/approve` | **FALSE** | `grep -n '/approve' docs/OVERSIGHT-RUNBOOK.md` → **no matches.** Found by the panel (P-3); **re-derived by me this session** (§0.0.3) |
| *"the `merge_authority.py` `_HUMAN_GATE_LABELS` merge-block removal"* | **FALSE** | `_HUMAN_GATE_LABELS` blocks on **PR** labels (`scripts/automation/lib/merge_authority.py:494`, `:519-521`, `:557-566`); `/approve` fires **only on issues** (`label-swap.yml:29` excludes `github.event.issue.pull_request`). **A command that cannot touch a PR's labels cannot be the thing that clears a PR's merge block.** The architect's finding; **re-derived independently by me** (§0.0.3) |
| *"the documented triage transition"* | **True, but not a dependency** | `docs/LABELS.md:29` documents `/approve` as a `needs-ai` writer, and `worker.md`/`worker-cron-prompt.md` cite its swap *pattern* as the model for the PR-side clear. Those are citations of a **pattern**, not callers. **No code invokes `/approve`** — and S2 invalidates the transition anyway |

**I stated three reasons to leave a live workflow alone. Two do not exist, and the third is a documentation reference to a transition S2 invalidates.** Both false ones were checkable in one `grep` each. The lesson I am recording against myself, because it generalises past this instance: **the two false reasons were the two I did not verify, and they were false in the direction that supported the conclusion I had already reached.** The third — the one I *had* read — is the one that survived, narrowed. A stated reason is not evidence; it is a claim with a citation attached, and this document's own §0 discipline applies to its dispositions and not only to its facts.

**No new protected surface.** `.github/workflows/**` is already in S2's five (AM-17) and `label-swap.yml` was already being edited for AM-6 point 3. **This changes *what* that edit is, not how many surfaces S2 touches. The count remains five.**

**Disclosed, not absorbed.** Making a command a CODEOWNER types behave differently is **user-visible behaviour** for the one human this system has, so per the CORE product-boundary checkpoint it is **named to the human at H1** (Amendment 3 §3(b) item 2's fourth bullet and item 4's fifth surface) rather than bound silently. **If they want `/approve` to keep swapping labels, they say so at H1 and the wording-only mitigation is taken instead** — the security properties are identical either way, because `/approve` cannot authorize in S2 under any version. But they should decide it knowing that leaving it live currently takes away the cheap path to authorization.

**What is unchanged: S2 remains event-only.** AM-6 point 2 is explicit that comment-reading must not be bolted onto the critical-path fix, and I am not proposing it. Making `/approve` inert is the *opposite* of pulling its channel forward: it removes a write, it adds no read, and it leaves the gate's input set exactly as AM-6 point 2 specifies.

**Ruled in revision 3: comment-reading is NOT pulled forward (AM-16 — E-5 question 3).** I raised it because E-5 is the strongest argument for AM-6 point 4 that exists — `label-swap.yml:29` has no label-state precondition, so a `/approve` comment is a **single** act available on an already-labelled issue, which is exactly the act the cutover set lacks. The architect verified that and held the line anyway, for three reasons in ascending weight, and they are recorded here because a future reader will re-discover the same argument:

1. **The cost that motivated the question is gone.** AM-14 replaces a ≥186-act pre-merge pass with a single-digit primed head and a lazy drain (§2.7.3). Two acts at the moment the human decides an issue is next — a decision they already make — is not a burden that justifies advancing a new authorization channel onto the critical path.
2. **The comment channel is irrevocable by construction, and its bounds are S3's content.** Every event-derived authorization has a natural clearing act (`unlabeled`, `demilestoned` — which is what AM-8's S3 rule formalises). **A comment has no inverse:** there is no "uncomment" event, and a deleted comment leaves no timeline record the gate can read. A `/approve` typed months ago on an issue since rewritten would authorize it indefinitely, and the two mechanisms that bound that — AD-5's digest binding and its ordering invariant (approval newer than assessment) — are precisely what S3 introduces. Shipping the channel in S2 and its bounds in S3 means S3 retrofits invariants onto live authorization semantics instead of adding a channel already inside them. **The channel and its bounds are one design; the ordering is not an accident of the build plan.**
3. **Decisive: it would put a newly-specified authorization channel on the critical-path fix, before the panel has examined the channels that already exist.** The comment channel's whole correctness rests on anchoring to the GitHub-reported comment author, and the failure mode of getting that wrong is an issue's own body claiming its approval — the **same class of fact as AF-5**, a four-line `env:` block that silently decided whether an authorization path worked, unnoticed through an ADR, a design and five days. A second instance of that class does not belong in the commit that closes the first one.

**Consequence for S3, recorded now so the reason is on the record when it is built rather than reconstructed: E-5 is added to the S3 work item as the ergonomic justification for AM-6 point 4.** AM-6 point 4 is unchanged and remains binding on S3, with the anchoring requirement, the untrusted-body rule, and the one-extra-API-call cost exactly as stated.

**AM-6 point 3 remains a shipping condition:** *S2 MUST NOT ship while the repo tells a human that `/approve` authorizes work.* **Revision 4 satisfies it by removing the act rather than by correcting the claim.** Three required elements of the S2 change set, all listed in §2.1 — the third is new:

| Where | Required change |
|---|---|
| `docs/LABELS.md` | The `needs-ai` row's writer column already names `label-swap.yml` (`/approve`, `/handoff`) — verified in revision 2, re-read in revision 3. **Updated in revision 4: `/approve` is no longer a writer of `needs-ai` at all**, and the row must say so rather than describing a swap that no longer happens. It gains: that in the S2 era `/approve` **changes no labels** and authorizes nothing; the acts that **do** authorize (§2.7.1, §2.7.4 verbatim); AM-15's authorization-record sentence; and — **new in revision 4, AM-27 point 2** — **the milestone-route prohibition**, with the Step 0 race as its stated reason. §4.3 carries the full wording requirement. **This file is the durable reference home for the authorizing act.** |
| `.github/workflows/label-swap.yml:74-75` | **Changed in revision 4.** `:74`'s `gh issue edit --remove-label needs-human --add-label needs-ai … 2>/dev/null \|\| true` is **removed**. `:75`'s body — currently `"✅ /approve — needs-human → needs-ai (authorized by @${COMMENTER})."` — is replaced by a statement that **`/approve` does not authorize autonomous work and has changed nothing**, naming the act that does. The **three facts** it must carry are now (a) this authorized nothing, (b) **no label was changed** — which replaces revision 3's "it may have consumed your additive act", since it no longer can, and (c) here is what does authorize, from your own account. **Exact wording is the coder's** (the architect declined to fix it, and declines again in Amendment 3 §4). §6.3 pins the **absence** of `authorized by` **and the presence** of a "does not authorize" statement — R4's hole, deliberately left open in one direction only. |
| `docs/OVERSIGHT-RUNBOOK.md:70-75` | **NEW in revision 4 — AM-25 / panel P-3.** The **operator's procedure** home. §4.3 carries the three facts. |

**Correction to the panel's P-3, which sharpens it rather than weakening it — and it is why the runbook fix must carry three things, not one.** The panel said the runbook's act is a no-op because the issue *"already carries `needs-ai` — which is every issue in the cutover set."* That is right for the queued set, but **the runbook passage is about a different case**: the overseer's escalation handoff, where the issue may carry only `needs-human`. On *that* issue, *"remove `needs-human`, add `needs-ai`"* performed **from the human's own account** is a perfectly valid authorization — it is §2.7.1's act, described without its load-bearing qualifier. **So the runbook's defect is not only "sometimes a no-op": it is that it never says the act must be performed from the human's own account**, in a repo whose documented tooling for label edits is `bootstrap/edit_issue.sh --app human`, which writes as `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` and which AM-1 consequence 1 forbids as an approval path. **The failure is therefore available on *every* issue, not only on those already carrying the label.**

**There is a good precedent sitting in the same file to align with, and the fix should read as its sibling rather than as a new concept.** The release-request exception (NG3b) at `:77` onward — read by me this session — **already teaches a human act whose *actor* is verified from the GitHub Events API, in those words**: *"your removal of it is one of the three authorization signals the worker verifies from the GitHub Events API"*, performed *"as the same CODEOWNER account, directly in GitHub."* That paragraph is the shape the corrected control 1 should take. A reader who has absorbed NG3b already understands why the account matters; the fix is to stop making control 1 the one place in the file that omits it.

**`/approve` in the S2 era is INERT, which supersedes revision 3's "actively harmful" characterisation — but the harm it describes is why the change was made, so the reasoning is retained.** Revision 2 documented `/approve` as a no-op for authorization; **AM-15 hardened that to actively harmful**, on the mechanical finding that `label-swap.yml:29` has **no label-state precondition** — the command fires on any issue — so on an issue not yet carrying the dispatch label, `/approve` applied that label as `github-actions[bot]`, **consuming the one genuinely additive act the human still had** and converting a one-act issue into a two-act issue (§2.7.4). Revision 3 could only document that. **Revision 4 removes it: with no label write, there is no act to consume.** A reader encountering AM-15's "actively harmful" language in this chain should read it as the *diagnosis that motivated AM-25*, not as a live property of the shipped command.

**What S3 does about it (AM-6 point 4, stated now so it is not redesigned from scratch):** S3's gate accepts, in addition to the event sources, **a comment whose GitHub-reported author is a verified human CODEOWNER and whose first line begins with the approval command**, anchored the same way `label-swap.yml` already anchors it (first word of the first line). The comment *body* stays untrusted content — only the anchored command token and the API-reported author are read, and nothing in the body may influence the outcome. This costs one additional API call per untrusted candidate, on the path the ADR's cost note already accepts as non-free. The attack to watch, and the one the panel should press on: an implementation that reads "the gate reads comments" and forgets the anchoring on the *GitHub-reported author*, which would let an issue's own body claim its approval.

### 2.7.3 The cutover set: bounded by the gate's own query, no grandfathering (AM-3)

**A grandfathering list is forbidden.** It is two violations at once: a static list of pre-approved issues is a trusted-set widening (AD-13) *and* a cached eligibility determination (AD-4/FR7 — an authorization verified at time T is re-verified at T+n; an approval recorded earlier is evidence to re-check, not a stored grant). Revision 1 argued against it; AM-3 makes it a rule.

**The cutover set is bounded by the gate's own query — and it is at least 97 issues (updated in revision 4).** The boundary rule is unchanged and still right: an issue that is closed, milestone-less, in a non-target milestone, or without the dispatch label costs nothing at cutover — it costs one human act at the moment it would otherwise have been selected, which is the same act the human already performs implicitly when they decide it is next. The set is therefore **the open, dispatch-labelled, non-PR issues in the active milestone**, exactly `--milestone <n>&labels=needs-ai&state=open` minus `EXCLUDED_LABELS` minus PR records.

**What revision 2 got wrong here, corrected by AF-6 and cited rather than re-derived.** Revision 2 wrote "the cutover set is not 46 issues" and AM-3 wrote "far smaller than 46". Both inferred *smallness* from a boundary rule, and bounding a set by a query says nothing about its cardinality. Measured live by the architect with the exact query AM-3 names as the bound:

```
bash bootstrap/query_issues.sh --app worker --list --milestone "v0.7.0" --label needs-ai --state open
  → 2026-09-14 (panel):     96 records, #1539 at position 86
  → 2026-09-15 (architect): 97 records, #1539 at position 88
  → 2026-09-16 (me, §0.0.3): 97 records, #1539 at position 88
```

**Updated in revision 4. The figure is 97, and the three measurements above are the whole of its provenance** — the 2026-09-16 row is mine, re-derived this session through the sanctioned read path; the other two are cited from the panel and Amendment 3 §0 respectively and are **not** re-derived by me. Revision 3's **≥93** was AF-6's measurement and was correct when made.

**The movement between those rows is AF-8's finding and matters more than the figure.** A `created desc` list moves old issues toward the truncation edge **monotonically as new issues are filed**. In two days the page gained a record and #1539 moved two places further from the head. **It is roughly a dozen filings from falling off a 100-record page entirely** — which is why §2.2.1 paginates Step C and why R-6 is closed rather than carried (§7.2). A residual recorded without its distance to firing is a residual nobody can prioritise.

**Read the number with both of its error bars, because they point in opposite directions and both matter:**

- **It is a floor.** `query_issues.sh --list` issues a single `per_page=100` page with **no** `--paginate` (`:189-201`, read by me this session), then filters PR records. 97 non-PR records could be a full 100-record page containing 3 PRs — and PRs in this repo do carry `needs-ai`. **I can bound page occupancy at ≥97/100 but cannot state it exactly**, because `--list` filters PRs before printing. So **≥97, possibly more**, and any document quoting "97" without "at least" is quoting it wrong. **Note the irony and do not let it pass unremarked: the tool the human is told to enumerate their queue with has exactly the defect AM-22 just removed from the gate.** It is not fixed here — S2 must not acquire another thing to carry — but the human's own enumeration remains page-limited after S2 ships, and §4.3 requires the command to be presented with that caveat attached.
